power_id text
1 Following Lohse and Waichman (2020), each generational sequence comprised four generations, and each condition started with 18 first-generation groups. Only participants in the first three generations actually made decisions, and the next generation was recruited only if the previous generation had passed the pool on; after the third generation had made its decisions, its contributions determined whether the pool would be passed on to the fourth generation, for which no participants were recruited. A total of 183 participants were included in the anonymous and future-generation decision disclosure (hereafter, future-generation disclosure) conditions: 75 in the anonymous condition (69 women; Mage = 20.27 years, SD = 1.48) and 108 in the futuregeneration disclosure condition (94 women; Mage = 20.10 years, SD = 1.18). Because the total sample size depended on how many pools were passed on and could not be fixed in advance, we conducted a sensitivity power analysis instead of an a priori power analysis in G*Power 3.1 (Faul et al., 2007 (Faul et al.,, 2009; ANOVA: Fixed effects, special, main effects and interactions). With α = .05, a total sample size of N = 183, four groups, and a numerator df of 1, the design had 80% power to detect effects of at least f = 0.21 (approximately ηp² = .04) for the main effect of disclosure condition and its interaction with SVO; with 90% and 95% power, the minimum detectable effects were f = 0.24 and 0.27, respectively. Because cell sizes were unequal, these values are approximate. All participants had normal or corrected-to-normal vision and no history of psychiatric disorders. They signed an informed consent form before the experiment and were paid after it. The study was approved by the local ethics committee. Data were collected in spring 2024.
2 Participants in the anonymous and peer disclosure conditions were recruited in spring 2024 in the same recruitment wave as those in the futuregeneration disclosure condition of Experiment 1, whereas participants in the name-and-face disclosure condition were recruited separately in November 2024 (data collection ended on November 27). A sensitivity power analysis was again conducted in G*Power 3.1 (ANOVA:
3 Participants in Experiment 2 were recruited and selected in the same way as in Experiment 1. The first generation again consisted of 18 groups, and the next generation was recruited only if the previous generation had passed the pool on; three generations were actually run. In total, 354 participants were included in the anonymous, peer decision disclosure (hereafter, peer disclosure), and peer name-and-face disclosure (hereafter, name-and-face disclosure) conditions: 75 in the anonymous condition (69 women; Mage = 20.27 years, SD = 1.48; these participants were drawn from Experiment 1), 132 in the peer disclosure condition Fixed effects, special, main effects and interactions). With α = .05, a total sample size of N = 354, six groups, and a numerator df of 2, the design had 80% power to detect effects of at least f = 0.17 (approximately ηp² = .03), which can serve as an approximate reference for the three-level main effect and its interaction with the other factor; with 90% and 95% power, the minimum detectable effects were approximately f = 0.19 and 0.21, respectively. Because cell sizes were unequal, these values are approximate. Because of the different recruitment waves, participants in the name-and-face disclosure condition were older than those in the other two conditions, F(2, 350) = 103.19, p < .001, ηp² = .371. The proportion of women also differed across the three conditions (92.0%, 83.3%, and 70.1%, respectively), χ 2 (2, N = 354) = 16.50, p < .001, V = .22.

Power analyses need to contain the following information to be interpretable: the type of power analysis, the statistical test, the software used, sample size, critical alpha criterion, power level, effect size, and an effect size metric. In addition, it is recommended to make sure the power analysis is reproducible (by sharing the code, or a screenshot, of the power analysis), and to provide good arguments for why the study was designed to detect an effect of this size.

For an a-priori power analysis, where the sample size is determined, reporting all information would look like:

An a priori power analysis for an independent samples t-test, conducted using the pwr.t.test function from pwr (Champely, 2020), indicated that for a Cohen’s d = 0.5, an alpha level of 0.05, and a desired power level of 80% required at least 64 participants in each group.

For a sensitivity power analysis, this sentence would look like:

A sensitivity power analysis for an independent samples t-test, conducted using the pwr.t.test function from pwr (Champely, 2020), indicated that with 64 participants in each group, and an alpha level of 0.05, a desired power level of 80% was reached for an effect size of d = 0.5.

To fix Exact P-Values

What we found

We found 2 imprecise p values out of 65 detected p values.

Show details

Reporting p values imprecisely (e.g., p < .05) reduces transparency, reproducibility, and re-use (e.g., in p value meta-analyses). Best practice is to report exact p-values with three decimal places (e.g., p = .032) unless p values are smaller than 0.001, in which case you can use p < .001.

This module sometimes flags false positives when sentences discuss how p-value thresholds were set. It can also miss an imprecise p-value that is reported against a threshold (e.g., “decreased at the p < .05 significance threshold”).

P-Value Text
ps > .23 Further interaction contrasts (Bonferroni corrected, k = 3) showed that the gap in contributions between prosocial and proself individuals was significantly smaller in the name-and-face disclosure condition than in the anonymous condition (difference in gaps = 1.21, adjusted 95% CI [0.18, 2.23], t(348) = 2.84, p = .014); none of the other pairwise comparisons were significant (ps > .23).
ps > .25 Post hoc comparisons showed that only the name-and-face disclosure condition (4.5%) had a significantly lower proportion than the anonymous condition (23.1%), χ 2 (1, N = 128) = 10.26, p = .004, V = .28; none of the other pairwise comparisons were significant (ps > .25).

The APA manual states: Report exact p values (e.g., p = .031) to two or three decimal places. However, report p values less than .001 as p < .001. However, 2 decimals is too imprecise for many use-cases (e.g., a p value meta-analysis), so report p values with three digits.

American Psychological Association (2020). Publication manual of the American Psychological Association, 7 edition. American Psychological Association.

README Files

README files are a way to document the contents and structure of a folder, helping users locate the information they need. You can use a README to document changes to a repository, and explain how files are named. Please consider adding a README to each repository or including ‘README’ in the name of your overview document.

Files that do not belong in the repository

We left out 73 files that software creates by itself, not the authors: operating system (72), R history (1). They hold nothing a reader can use, but they clutter the repository, and software can mistake them for real files (for example a ._ copy of a .csv for data).

Remove these: Operating system files: macOS adds a __MACOSX folder and a ._ copy of every file it puts in a zip, and .DS_Store files to folders; Windows adds Thumbs.db and desktop.ini. On a Mac, create zips from the command line with zip -r -X, or remove them afterwards with zip -d archive.zip "__MACOSX/*" "*.DS_Store". RStudio session files (.Rproj.user/) hold open tabs and unsaved edits; R history (.Rhistory) lists the commands typed in a session and can hold file paths and passwords; Jupyter checkpoints (.ipynb_checkpoints/) are automatic copies of notebooks; compiled Python files (__pycache__/, .pyc) are recreated when the code runs; open-document lock files (~$...docx, .~lock...#) are left while a document is open; editor backups (file~, .bak, .swp) and temporary files (.tmp, .temp) are copies a program made by itself.

Keep only on purpose: version control folders (.git, .svn, .hg) hold the project’s history, which can include earlier versions of files and data that was later removed; editor settings (.idea, .vscode) can hold paths on your own computer. A .gitignore based on GitHub’s templates (https://github.com/github/gitignore) keeps them out of a repository.

To check Non-Significant P Value Check

What we found

We found 26 non-significant p values that should be checked for appropriate interpretation.

Show details

Meta-scientific research has shown nonsignificant p values are commonly misinterpreted. It is incorrect to infer that there is ‘no effect’, ‘no difference’, or that groups are ‘the same’ after p > 0.05.

It is possible that there is a true non-zero effect, but that the study did not detect it. Make sure your inference acknowledges that it is possible that there is a non-zero effect. It is correct to include the effect is ‘not significantly’ different, although this just restates that p > 0.05.

Metacheck does not yet analyze automatically whether sentences which include non-significant p-values are correct, but we recommend manually checking the sentences below for possible misinterpreted non-significant p values.

For metascientific articles demonstrating the rate of misinterpretations of non-significant results is high, see:

Aczel B, Palfi B, Szollosi A, Kovacs M, Szaszi B, Szecsi P, Zrubka M, Gronau Q, van den Bergh D, Wagenmakers E (2018). “Quantifying Support for the Null Hypothesis in Psychology: An Empirical Investigation.” Advances in Methods and Practices in Psychological Science, 1(3), 357–366. doi:10.1177/2515245918773742.

Murphy S, Merz R, Reimann L, Fernández A (2025). “Nonsignificance misinterpreted as an effect’s absence in psychology: Prevalence and temporal analyses.” Royal Society Open Science, 12(3), 242167. doi:10.1098/rsos.242167.

For educational material on preventing the misinterpretation of p values, see Improving Your Statistical Inferences.

Absolute Paths

Best programming practice is to use relative file paths (e.g., ‘./files’) instead of absolute file paths (e.g., ‘C://Lakens/project_dir/files’) as these folder names do not exist on other computers. Working Directory (setwd)

Best programming practice is to avoid setwd() in analysis code: it hardcodes an assumption about the working directory (often an absolute path on the author’s own machine), so the code breaks when run anywhere else. Keep the working directory as the caller sets it and use relative paths. No setwd() calls were found in any of the code files.

Package Installation (install.packages)

Best programming practice is to avoid install.packages() in analysis code: it installs software on whoever runs the script without asking, which is at minimum disruptive (an unexpected install, network access, a package version the author never tested against) and at worst a security risk if it points at an untrusted repository. Document required packages instead (a README, or a list of them: an renv.lock/DESCRIPTION for R, a requirements.txt for Python). No install.packages() calls were found in any of the code files.

Libraries / Imports

Best programming practice is to load all required libraries/imports in one block near the top of the code. In all code files, libraries/imports were loaded in one block.

Packages / Dependencies

The code files load 13 distinct packages/libraries: car, dplyr, effectsize, emmeans, ggplot2, matplotlib, numpy, os, pandas, PIL, psych, scipy, tidyr. These are the names found in the source (no version information is available from static analysis).

Reproducible Environment

The repository pins its analysis environment via a sessionInfo() record. Declared R version: 4.4.1. The sessionInfo() record lists 50 package versions.

No list of the Python packages the code needs (a requirements.txt, environment.yml, Pipfile.lock, poetry.lock, uv.lock or pyproject.toml) was found anywhere in the repository. Without one, the package versions used for the analysis are not recoverable, and they may drift between when this analysis was run and any later reproduction attempt. Consider depositing a requirements.txt that lists each package with its version (for example the output of pip freeze).

This module runs automated data-quality checks (empty columns, SPSS filter variables, category casing, stray whitespace, numbers stored as text, problematic and colliding column names, mixed text encodings, personal information, spreadsheet formatting, and careless responding) on the extracted data files.

We examined 195 columns across 20 data files.

Careless Responding

1 distinct respondent was flagged for straightlining: giving the same answer for at least 80% of the items in a multi-item scale of 5 items or more. One respondent can be flagged in several scales; the table below is one row per person, and the Longest run column gives the run that triggered the strongest flag, so you can check it against the raw data.

Of these, 1 were flagged only by short-scale straightlining (a run of identical answers on a scale of 7 items or fewer). On a short, one-directional scale, answering consistently is often normal, coherent responding rather than carelessness — treat these as weak signals and inspect the actual responses before excluding anyone.

These are prompts to inspect those rows, not definitive judgements.

Respondent Blocks flagged Scales Longest run Short-scale only
95 1 q (1-9, 6 items) same answer 6 times in a row, out of 6 items (flagged at 5) yes

What this does not cover. Only 2 of 20 data files could be screened: the rest had no detectable multi-item scale block, or too few respondents. Even in a screened file, these checks only look at multi-item rating scales — the only place where a repeated or flat answer pattern is interpretable. Single questions, open text, demographics and any item outside a detected scale block are never examined. A respondent can therefore answer most of a survey carelessly and still not be flagged here, and finding nothing is not evidence that a dataset is free of careless responding.

How to make your code run on another computer

We ran your code on a clean computer, the way a reader or reviewer would. Below is each problem we found, the scripts it affects, and how to fix it. Fix them in the order given: a problem near the top can cause the ones below it.

A file is only inside a zip file.

  • make_trend3.py reads /tmp/Downloads/analysis_code/data/Final_E1.csv, which is inside data_and_analysis_code.zip.
  • make_tokens.py reads /tmp/Downloads/data_and_analysis_code/data/Final_E1.csv, which is inside data_and_analysis_code.zip.

Code cannot read a file inside an archive until someone unpacks it. Either upload the files unpacked, in the folders the script expects, or start the script with a line that unpacks the archive, such as unzip(“Data.zip”) in R.

Comparison with the paper

Of the 52 statistical tests reported in the paper, 26 were found in the output of the code.

All details of the Reproducibility Check

This module assesses whether the paper’s code could be run on its data, and — because execute = TRUE — actually ran it. We examined 5 R code files.

Run order

The scripts were ordered by their data dependencies (a script writing a file another reads runs first), source() calls, and numeric filename prefixes.

Order File Runs after Basis
1 00_import_and_cleaning.R — numeric
2 01_experiment1.R 00_import_and_cleaning.R dependency
3 02_experiment2.R 00_import_and_cleaning.R dependency
4 03_supplementary_analyses.R 00_import_and_cleaning.R dependency
5 run_all.R 00_import_and_cleaning.R dependency

File paths

1 data path in the code does not work in the repository as deposited. We pointed it to where the file is, so the code could run: Final_E2.csv to data/Final_E2.csv (in 00_import_and_cleaning.R). The code should be changed, because as written it does not find the file.

Missing inputs

2 input files the code reads are in the deposit but not usable as named: too large to download, inside an archive, or under a similar name.

File Status Reason
face_assesment.csv in_archive referenced file is only inside data_and_analysis_code.zip; unpack it so the code finds it
final_e1.csv in_archive referenced file is only inside data_and_analysis_code.zip; unpack it so the code finds it

Execution

The code was run. Each script ran in an isolated Docker container against a throwaway copy of the repository, in the run order above, with a 300-second per-script timeout. Of 5 scripts: 4 ran without error, 0 errored, 0 timed out, 0 could not run because one of its own dependencies is unavailable, 0 did not parse, and 1 was run by another script with source(). Running against current package versions: a break can reflect version drift, which argues for pinning versions. 3 scripts stopped on a file another script writes, so they were run once more after the other scripts: 02_experiment2.R (Final_E2.csv); 03_supplementary_analyses.R (Final_E2.csv); run_all.R (Final_E2.csv). Then these ran to the end: 02_experiment2.R, 03_supplementary_analyses.R, run_all.R. Which script must run first can be stated in a README, or shown by numbering the file names (01_, 02_). A script looked for a file in another folder than the deposit has it, so before running it once more we copied it there: data_and_analysis_code.zip/data_and_analysis_code/data/Final_E2.csv to data_and_analysis_code.zip/data_and_analysis_code/data/data/Final_E2.csv for 01_experiment1.R. Building paths from the top of the project (as with here::here()), or keeping the folders the code expects, solves this.

File Outcome Detail Time (s)
00_import_and_cleaning.R run_by_source 0.0
01_experiment1.R ran_ok 3.7
02_experiment2.R ran_ok 4.0
03_supplementary_analyses.R ran_ok 3.8
run_all.R ran_ok 4.7

Calls that install packages in the code

1 call to install.packages(), groundhog, checkpoint, renv, pacman and the like was replaced, since they install packages themselves: groundhog.library() and p_load() by library(), the others by a statement that does nothing. The scripts ran with the packages installed before the run.

Run by another script

1 script is run by another script with source(), so it was not run on its own: each script runs in a fresh R process, and on its own a sourced script lacks the packages and objects the sourcing script sets up first. Its outcome is part of the sourcing script’s outcome.

> followups <- function(model) {
>   pw  <- pairs_tbl(emmeans(model, ~ disc), model)
>   se  <- as.data.frame(joint_tests(model, by = "svo"))
>   se  <- se[se$`model term` == "disc", ]
>   pws <- pairs_tbl(emmeans(model, ~ disc | svo), model)
>   pws <- pws[pws$svo == "Proself", ]
>   gap <- pairs_tbl(emmeans(model, ~ svo | disc), model, adjust = "none", reverse = FALSE)
>   ic  <- as.data.frame(summary(contrast(emmeans(model, ~ svo * disc),
>                                         interaction = c("pairwise", "pairwise"),
>                                         adjust = "bonferroni")))
>   rbind(
>     data.frame(family = "sans", comparison = pw$contrast,
>                statistic = "Mdiff", value = pw$estimate, p = pw$p.value),
>     data.frame(family = "sans", comparison = paste("Within", se$svo),
>                statistic = "F", value = se$F.ratio, p = se$p.value),
>     data.frame(family = "sans", comparison = pws$contrast,
>                statistic = "Mdiff", value = pws$estimate, p = pws$p.value),
>     data.frame(family = "sans", comparison = as.character(gap$disc),
>                statistic = "Mdiff", value = gap$estimate, p = gap$p.value),
>     data.frame(family = "sans", comparison = gsub("[()]", "", ic$disc_pairwise),
>                statistic = "Difference", value = ic$estimate, p = ic$p.value))
> }
> E2_age <- filter(E2, !is.na(age))
> models <- list(
>   "Original model"           = lm(tokens ~ disc * svo, data = E2),
>   "Model 1 (gender)"         = lm(tokens ~ disc * svo + male, data = E2),
>   "Model 2 (gender and age)" = lm(tokens ~ disc * svo + male + age, data = E2_age))
> table_S3 <- list()
> table_S4 <- list()

Dependencies were installed into a throwaway library before running: 7 succeeded, 0 failed. car, dplyr, effectsize, emmeans, ggplot2, psych, tidyr were installed.

Python code

This module also assesses whether the paper’s Python code could be run on its data, and — because execute = TRUE — actually ran it. We examined 3 Python code files.

Run order

The scripts were ordered by their data dependencies (a script writing a file another reads runs first) and numeric filename prefixes. Python has no source()-equivalent import-based ordering signal (an import loads a module, not the paper’s own analysis flow), so that basis never applies here.

Order File Runs after Basis
1 make_trend3.py — numeric
2 make_strategy.py — none
3 make_tokens.py — none

File paths

Every data path in the code works in the repository as deposited.

Execution

The Python code was run. Each script ran in an isolated Docker container against a throwaway copy of the repository, in the run order above, with a 300-second per-script timeout. Of 3 scripts: 1 ran without error, 2 errored, 0 timed out, 0 could not run because one of its own dependencies is unavailable, 0 did not parse, and 0 are written for Python 2 and not run. Running against current package versions: a break can reflect version drift, which argues for pinning versions (e.g. in a requirements.txt).

Ethics Approval Check

Based on the following text, we would expect an ethics approval statement, and it was present:

In Experiment 1, disclosing to the next generation what participants had contributed increased the rate of sustained generations but did not reliably increase individual contributions or narrow the gap between prosocial and proself individuals.

In the future-generation decision disclosure condition, following previous manipulations of reputational cues (Arfer et al., 2015), participants were told before making their contribution decisions that their decisions would be disclosed to >Method

Only participants in the first three generations actually made decisions, and the next generation was recruited only if the previous generation had passed the pool on; after the third generation had made its decisions, its contributions determined whether the pool would be passed on to the fourth generation, for which no participants were recruited.

A total of 183 participants were included in the anonymous and future-generation decision disclosure (hereafter, future-generation disclosure) conditions: 75 in the anonymous condition (69 women; Mage = 20.27 years, SD = 1.48) and 108 in the futuregeneration disclosure condition (94 women; Mage = 20.10 years, SD = 1.18).

All participants had normal or corrected-to-normal vision and no history of psychiatric disorders.

Following the classification criteria of Murphy et al. (2011), participants with SVO angles ≥ 22.45° were classified as prosocial and those with angles < 22.45° as proself; in this experiment, two participants were competitive (< -12.04°) and were included in the proself category.

The task was presented as a generational sequence: Participants were told that each sequence involved 12 participants randomly assigned to four three-person groups, that each group represented one generation, and that the generations would make their decisions one after another.

Participants were told that the tokens they kept would be converted into payment at a fixed rate.

Unlike Lohse and Waichman (2020), who informed participants of their position in the generational sequence, we did not reveal to participants which generation they were in; instead, all participants were told that their group was neither the first nor the last.

In addition, all participants were told that their final earnings depended on the decisions of the previous group and on their own decisions, and that their own decisions would also affect the earnings of the next group.

Before the task, participants were explicitly told that their individual contributions would be kept completely confidential.

Before the task, participants were likewise explicitly told that their individual contributions, together with those of the other two members, would be shown to the three members of the next generation.

Participants in Experiment 2 were recruited and selected in the same way as in Experiment 1.

In total, 354 participants were included in the anonymous, peer decision disclosure (hereafter, peer disclosure), and peer name-and-face disclosure (hereafter, name-and-face disclosure) conditions: 75 in the anonymous condition (69 women; Mage = 20.27 years, SD = 1.48; these participants were drawn from Experiment 1), 132 in the peer disclosure condition Fixed effects, special, main effects and interactions).

Age data for one participant were treated as missing because of an entry error, so age statistics are based on 353 participants.

The face stimuli were prepared following the general procedure of Gong et al. (2011): Eight undergraduates (four men and four women) were recruited from a university, and photographs of their neutral facial expressions were taken under standardized conditions.

The difference was that, in the peer disclosure and name-and-face disclosure conditions, participants were told that their decisions and related information would be disclosed to the other two members of their group.

Before making their contribution decisions, participants were told that, after this round of contributions, their contribution, name, and photograph would be disclosed to the other two group members and that they would also see the other two members’ results; they were then shown an example of the disclosure screen, which displayed two faces (Faces 2 and 4), with the names and contributions replaced by the placeholders “Zhang San” and “Li Si” and “X” and “Y,” respectively.

Because future-generation and peer disclosure were tested against a shared anonymous baseline, the contrast between them is indirect, and the two audiences differed in more than their presence at the time of decision: peers were co-actors who faced the same choice, whose own contributions were also disclosed, and with whom participants might have expected further contact, whereas the next generation consisted of recipients whom participants had no reason to expect to meet.

A second limitation concerns the sample: given the constraints, participants were recruited in two waves and had an uneven age and gender composition, and a broader sample is needed to test the boundaries of these conclusions.

Participants in the anonymous and peer disclosure conditions were recruited in spring 2024 in the same recruitment wave as those in the futuregeneration disclosure condition of Experiment 1, whereas participants in the name-and-face disclosure condition were recruited separately in November 2024 (data collection ended on November 27).

Participants in the anonymous and peer disclosure conditions were recruited in spring 2024 in the same recruitment wave as those in the future-generation disclosure condition of Experiment 1; participants in the name-and-face disclosure condition were recruited separately in November 2024 (see Section 3.1.1).

The study was approved by the local ethics committee.

Informed consent, payment, and ethics approval were the same as in Experiment 1.
