How Ethics Approval Check works

This module searches for statements of ethics approval, IRB approval, institutional review board approval, and related terms.

Patterns are designed to capture:

Human research committee names - Institutional Review Board / IRB (US standard) - Independent Ethics Committee / IEC (ICH-GCP international clinical trial term) - Ethical/Ethics Review Board (generic) - Ethics (Advisory/Review) Committee/Board/Panel/Sub-committee - Research Ethics Committee/Board/Panel (UK/Canada/Australia) - Medical Ethics Committee - Human Subjects/Research Committee / Human Research Ethics (Advisory) Panel - Committee for/on the Protection of Human Subjects - Committee on (Health) Research Ethics - Office of Research Ethics - Abbreviations: IEC, REC, REB, ERB, METC, DEC. REC/REB/ERB/DEC are short enough to collide with unrelated jargon (e.g. REC as a recognition-heuristic model abbreviation, DEC as a decision-task label, Reb as an author surname), so these four require an ethics/approval/committee/board/panel/institutional/ protocol word within ~30 characters in the same sentence. IRB and IEC are kept unconstrained as they are not observed to collide in practice. - Dutch: Medisch-ethische toetsingscommissie (METC) - French: Comité d'éthique / Comité d'Ethique

Animal research committee names - Institutional Animal Care and Use Committee / IACUC (US standard) - Any sentence containing animal(s) and committee (or board/panel) within ~5 words of each other — catches Experimental Animal Care and Use Committee, Animal Ethics Committee, Animal Welfare Board, Animal Welfare Ethical Review Body, etc. - UK: Animal Welfare Ethical Review Body / AWERB - Dutch: Dierexperimentencommissie / DEC (see DEC constraint above); Instantie voor Dierenwelzijn / IvD - German: Tierversuchskommission / TvK (advisory committee under §15 Tierschutzgesetz) - French: Comité d'éthique en expérimentation animale / CEEA; Comité d'éthique animale - Spanish: Comité de ética en experimentación animal / CEEA

Approval phrasing - Ethics/Ethical (approval|clearance) [was] (granted|obtained|received|given|secured) - Ethically approved - Approved by the (ethics|irb|institutional|independent|local|review|research) ... - This/The study/protocol/experiment/procedures/methods/research [was] (approved|reviewed) by - Study was ethically approved - (Followed|met|follow) the ethical (guidelines|standards|requirements) - In accordance with ... ethical (guidelines|standards) - Ethics protocol - Approved under (protocol|reference|number)

Waiver / exemption - Ethics/Ethical (waiver|exemption) - IRB (waiver|exemption|exempt) - Exempted from (ethics|irb|institutional review) - Waived by the (ethics|irb|institutional|review) (committee|board) - Deemed exempt

Declaration of Helsinki - Declaration of Helsinki / Helsinki Declaration

Patterns are NOT designed to capture: - General ethical considerations or ethical dilemmas in study content - Mentions of “ethics” in theoretical or philosophical contexts - Author approval of a manuscript (e.g. “all authors approved the final version”) - Ethical behaviour as a measured variable (e.g. “ethical decision-making”) - URLs containing “ethics” in path components

Live data collection is detected by the internal helper .detect_live_data(), which searches for sentences indicating that data were collected directly from human or animal participants. Papers without any live-data signal are not flagged as missing ethics approval. The helper uses conservative patterns to avoid flagging secondary data analyses, computational experiments, or economics papers that analyse existing survey data. See .detect_live_data() for the full list of patterns and intentional exclusions.

A paper that states that no human or animal subjects took part (“no human subjects were involved”, “did not involve human participants”, or people taking part “not as subjects”) is not expected to have an ethics statement, whatever participant phrase it also has (see .detect_no_participants()). A negation near a participant phrase alone (“No participants were excluded”) does not count: it reports a study with participants.

Read more in the metacheck manual.

How Power Analysis Check works

This module uses regular expressions to identify sentences that contain a statistical power analysis. If specified by the user, it also uses a large language model (LLM) to extract information reported in power analyses, including the statistical test, sample size, alpha level, desired level of power, and magnitude and type of effect size.

The Power Analysis Check module uses regular expressions to identify sentences that contain a statistical power analysis. Without the use of an LLM, the module uses regular expressions to classify the power analysis as a-priori, sensitivity or post-hoc. With the use of an LLM, it checks if the power analysis is reported with all required information.

The regular expressions can miss power analyses, or fail to classify them correctly. The type of power analysis is often difficult to classify, which can easily be solved by explicitly specifying the type of power analysis as ‘a-priori’, ‘sensitivity’, or ‘post-hoc’. Note that ‘post-hoc’ or ‘observed’ power is rarely useful. The LLM can fail to identify information in the paper, and will not have access to information in paragraphs in the paper other than those that contain the word ‘power’. This package was validated by the Metacheck team on articles in Psychological Science.

This module was developed by Lisa DeBruine, Daniel Lakens and Cristian Mesquida

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: In a sample of 128 papers with 246 instances of power statements, 203 were correctly detected (true positives), 22 were missed (false negatives) and 21 were incorrectly detected (false positives). Overall, among all instances flagged as power statements, 90.6% were correct (positive predictive value).

How Preregistration Check works

Retrieve information from preregistrations in a standardised way, and make them easier to check.

The Preregistration Check module identifies preregistrations on the OSF and AsPredicted based on links in the manuscript, retrieves the preregistration text, and organizes the information into a template. The module retrieves text from the OSF with its API, and from AsPredicted with ResearchBox’s API when a ResearchBox key is set (see rbox_token()), or else from the preregistration’s web page. The information in the preregistration is returned. An AsPredicted link that could not be read is named in the report, with the reason.

The module can’t extract information from non-structured preregistration templates (i.e., where the preregistration is uploaded in a single text field) and it can’t retrieve information in preregistrations that are stored as text documents on the OSF.

If you want to extend the package to be able to download information from other preregistration sites, reach out to the Metacheck development team.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Lisa DeBruine

Read more in the metacheck manual.

How Marginal Significance works

List all sentences that describe an effect as ‘marginally significant’.

The marginal module searches for regular expressions that match a predefined pattern. The list of terms is a subset of those listed in a blog post by Matthew Hankins. The module returns all sentences that match terms describing ‘marginally significant’ results.

Some of the terms identified might not be problematic in some contexts, and there are ways to describe ‘marginal significance’ that are not detected by the module.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: In a sample of 51 papers with 87 statements, this module correctly identified 38 statements (true positives) and incorrectly flagged 22 statements (false positives). It failed to detect 27 statements. Thus, among all statements flagged by the module, 63% were genuine cases (positive predictive value). However, the module missed 42% of all true statements (false negative rate).

How StatCheck works

Check consistency of p-values and test statistics

The Statcheck module runs Statcheck. Statcheck searches for regular expressions that match a predefined pattern, and identifies APA reported statistical tests. More information on the package can be found at https://github.com/cran/statcheck. The module only returns Statcheck results for t-tests and F-tests, as these are the only tests which have been validated, see https://osf.io/preprints/psyarxiv/tcxaj_v1/.

Statcheck was developed by Michèle Nuijten and Sascha Epskamp.

Statcheck considers p = 0.000 an error, as you should report p < 0.001. Furthermore, p < 0.03 is an error if the p-value was 0.031, and one should simply report exact p-values (p = 0.031). Statcheck might miss one-sided tests, and falsely assume the p-value is incorrect. For more information, see StatCheck.

This module only checks statistical results reported in the running text of the manuscript. It cannot (yet) process statistics reported only in tables.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Lisa DeBruine

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: In a sample of 685 tests with 34 instances of inconsistent reporting, Statcheck correctly detected 34 of them, and incorrectly identified 26. Therefore 0% of true instances were missed, and 43% of detections were false positives. See https://osf.io/preprints/psyarxiv/tcxaj_v1/ for more details of the validation.

How Exact P-Values works

List any p-values reported with insufficient precision (e.g., p < .05 or p = n.s.) or reported as exactly zero (e.g., p = .000).

This module uses regular expressions to identify p-values. It will flag any values reported as p > ? or p < numbers greater than .001. It will also flag p-values reported as exactly zero (e.g., p = .000, p = 0.00), which are mathematically impossible — p-values are never exactly zero and should instead be reported as p < .001.

A p-value that is not the result of a test is not flagged: a table note (“* p < .05, ** p < .01”), a figure legend (“Asterisks indicate significant differences (p < .05)”, “Values in bold are significant at p < .05”) or a stated threshold (“p < .05 was considered significant”, “thresholded at p < .001”). GROBID often puts such notes into the running text, so each p-value is judged by the words in its own clause (the text up to a “;” or a full stop), and never left out when a test statistic belongs to it (“F(1, 7) = 8.03, # p < .05”). The column not_a_test of the table says why a p-value was left out (“marker”, “threshold” or “legend”). Notes in other forms, such as a marker after the p-value (“p < .05 (*)“), are still flagged.

This module sometimes flags false positives when sentences discuss how p-value thresholds were set. It can also miss an imprecise p-value that is reported against a threshold (“decreased at the p < .05 significance threshold”).

This module only checks p-values reported in the running text of the manuscript. It cannot (yet) process p-values reported only in tables (as opposed to a table’s footnote text, which the module does see and tries to exclude — see above).

This module was developed by Lisa DeBruine and Daniel Lakens

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: In a sample of 225 papers containing 405 instances of non-exact p-values, the module correctly detected 269 cases (true positives) and incorrectly identified 78 (false positives). It missed 136 instances of imprecisely reported p-values (false negatives) and correctly identified 4557 cases of precisely reported p-values (true negative). Additionally, 78% of positive detections were correct (positive predictive value).

How Non-Significant P Value Check works

This module checks for imprecisely reported p values. If p > .05 is detected, it warns for misinterpretations.

The nonsignificant p-value check searches for regular expressions that match a predefined pattern. The module identifies all p-values in a manuscript and selects those that are not reported to be smaller than or equal to 0.05. It returns all sentences containing non-significant p-values.

In the future, the Metacheck team aims to incorporate a machine learning classifier to only return sentences likely to contain misinterpretations. If you want to help to improve the module, reach out to the Metacheck development team.

This module only checks p-values reported in the running text of the manuscript. It cannot (yet) process p-values reported only in tables.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: In a sample of 194 papers with 1602 instances of non-significant p-values, this module correctly detected 1486 of them, and incorrectly identified 153. Additionally, 91% of detections were true instances (positive predictive value). That is, when this module flags non-significant p-values in a paper, it correctly identifies an issue 91% of the time.

How Effect Sizes in t-tests and F-tests works

The Effect Size module checks if effect sizes are correctly reported in t-tests and F-tests.

The Effect Size check searches for regular expressions that match typical ways in which effect sizes are reported. It subsequently checks different ways in which Cohen’s d, g, ηp2, and ωp2 can be computed against the reported value. If effects are missing, or might be incorrect, you the module provides a warning. The module was validated on APA reported statistical tests, and might miss effect sizes that were reported in other reporting styles. It was validated by the Metacheck team on papers published in Psychological Science.

This module only checks statistical results reported in the running text of the manuscript. It cannot (yet) process statistics reported only in tables.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Lisa DeBruine

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: In a sample of 161 papers with 1469 tests, this module correctly detected 1106 reported effect sizes (true positives) and correctly identified 295 cases where no effect size was present (true negatives). However, it missed 23 that were reported (false negatives), and incorrectly identified 45 effect sizes when none were reported (false positives). Among all instances detected by the module, 96% were true cases (positive predictive value). In a validation against 221 reported Cohen’s d effect sizes, it correctly indicated coherence in 218 cases (99%). In a validation against 485 partial eta-squared effect sizes, it correctly indicated coherence in 480 (99%)

How Codebook Check works

This module checks whether the data columns in a repository are documented in a codebook or README. It locates codebook/readme files, extracts the variable definitions they contain, matches those definitions against the data columns extracted by data_check, and reports documentation coverage: how many data columns are documented, and which documented variables are never used.

The Codebook Check module consumes the columns and file classification produced by data_check (and, transitively, repo_check). Files classified as codebook or readme are parsed with parse_codebook(), which reads structured tables (CSV/TSV/Excel with a variable-name column and a label column), embedded labels in haven files (SPSS/Stata/SAS), and plain text from rich-text formats (docx/pdf/rtf/odt). Embedded haven labels from data files are also harvested directly.

Each data column is then matched against the parsed variable definitions with match_column_labels(), using normalised-name matching with experiment-group scoping, haven-label priority, and rule-based label-equivalence merging.

Beyond the variable label, the module harvests the DDI-style per-variable properties a source supplies: the value labels / code list (the 1=“Strongly disagree”…5=“Strongly agree” mapping — from SPSS/Stata value labels and from a codebook “values”/“coding” column), the missing-value scheme (which codes denote missingness, from haven declared missings and labels that read as “refused”/“n/a”), and question text and columns when present. These are carried onto the matched data columns and exported into the Psych-DS variableMeasured (as a schema.org code list plus namespaced metacheck: fields).

The module defaults to rules-only when llm_use(FALSE). When llm_use(TRUE), three optional LLM tiers run: parsing unstructured codebook text that the rules could not handle, fuzzy-matching still-unlabelled columns to still-unmatched codebook variables, and merging conflicting label definitions into a single canonical label.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: This module has not been validated. All checks in the codebook_check module have unknown error rates. Carefully evaluate the output of this module. You can help improve this module by reporting an issue on GitHub.

How Repository Check works

This module retrieves information from repositories.

The Repository Check module lists files on the OSF, GitHub, ResearchBox, DSpace (PsychArchives and other legacy- and DSpace 7-based installations), Zenodo, Dataverse, Figshare, Dryad, ReShare, 4TU.ResearchData, Mendeley Data, DataONE (Arctic Data Center, KNB, and other member nodes), and LDbase based on links in the manuscript.

Listing a repository’s files never requires an API key, but some hosts answer faster or more completely with one: OSF allows 100 requests/hour unauthenticated versus 10,000/day with a token (see [osf_pat()]), and Dryad enforces a strict per-IP daily quota on repeated listing (see cache below). Dryad is a special case beyond rate limits: listing a Dryad dataset’s files works without a key, but actually downloading file CONTENTS from Dryad fails with HTTP 401 unless a key is set, even for a fully public dataset (see [dryad_pat()] or [dryad_auth()]). Since this module only lists files and never downloads their contents itself, that limitation applies to data_check and code_check (which do download content), not here – see their own documentation.

When a linked OSF page is a registration, its registered_from project (the one it was registered from, which the manuscript itself may never link directly) is also checked: if that project is public its files are listed under its own URL, and if it is closed or otherwise inaccessible the report flags it explicitly, since some registrations only describe their content rather than mirroring it.

If you want to extend the package to be able to download files from additional data repositories reach out to the Metacheck development team.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Lisa DeBruine

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: This module has not been validated. All checks in the repo_check module have unknown error rates. Carefully evaluate the output of this module. You can help improve this module by reporting an issue on GitHub.

How Code Check works

This module retrieves information from repositories checked by repo_check about code files (R, Python, SAS, SPSS, Stata, Mplus, MATLAB).

The Code Check module checks R (also R Markdown and Quarto), Python (also Jupyter notebooks), SAS, SPSS, Stata, Mplus, and MATLAB files, using regular expressions to check the code; JASP files are listed but not read. The language of a Quarto document or a notebook is read from the file itself (its engine or kernel), not from its extension. The regular expression search will detect the number of comments, the lines at which libraries/imports are loaded, attempts to detect absolute paths to files, setwd() and install.packages() calls, and lists files that are loaded, and checks if these files are in the repository; R code is also parsed. The module will return suggestions to improve the code if there are no comments, if libraries/imports are loaded in lines further than 4 lines apart, if files that are loaded are not in the repository, if absolute file paths, setwd() or install.packages() are found, and if R code cannot be parsed.

The regular expressions can miss information in code files, or falsely detect parts of the code as a fixed file path. Libraries/imports might be loaded in one block, even if there are more than 3 intermittent lines. The module was tried out internally on papers published in Psychological Science, but has not been formally validated. There might be valid reasons why some loaded files can’t be shared, but the module can’t evaluate these reasons, and always gives a warning.

The module also checks whether the repository pins the R/package versions the analysis actually depended on: an renv.lock file (parsed for the R version and every locked package + its version/source), a sessionInfo()/sessioninfo::session_info() text dump (matched by filename — sessionInfo.txt/session_info.txt and similar, or embedded in a README), or a groundhog::groundhog.library()/checkpoint::checkpoint() date-pin call in the code (a bare library(groundhog)/library(checkpoint) does not count — the pinning call itself must be present). When none of these is found, the report flags it as a reproducibility gap: package versions may drift between when the analysis was run and any later reproduction attempt.

If you want to extend the package to perform additional checks on code files, or make the checks work on other types of code files, reach out to the Metacheck development team.

Checking code requires downloading its file contents. For a paper whose repository is on Dryad, this never succeeds unless a Dryad API key is set: Dryad requires one to download file bytes, even from a fully public dataset (listing the repository’s files does not need one). Without a key, every Dryad-hosted code file is listed but unchecked, and a warning naming Dryad and ?dryad_pat/?dryad_auth is included among the download failures recorded in the manifest.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Raphael Merz

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: This module has not been validated. All checks in the code_check module have unknown error rates. Carefully evaluate the output of this module. You can help improve this module by reporting an issue on GitHub.

How Data Check works

This module classifies the files in a repository into semantic types (data, codebook, code, software, output, supplemental, readme, asset, other) and, for tabular data files, extracts each column’s type and summary statistics.

The Data Check module consumes the file list produced by repo_check (or any files added via local_path). Each file is classified with rule-based heuristics layered over metacheck’s file_category() and file_types: file names matching README / codebook patterns win first, then an extension crosswalk, then format-locked extension overrides. For every file classified as tabular data that is available locally, the module reads the file head, describes each column with data_col_facets() and computes summary statistics for numeric columns. Every sheet of a spreadsheet that holds a table is read: a file with several such sheets has its columns under source_file = "file.xlsx [sheet]", and the sheet column names the sheet of any spreadsheet with several sheets; a sheet of notes or a codebook is treated as documentation when the file has another table. Following DDI, a column is described by orthogonal facets rather than a single type: representation (numeric/text/datetime/code — how it is stored), measurement_level (Stevens: nominal/ordinal/interval/ratio), concept (what it measures: reaction_time/age/gender/likert/id/…), role (identifier/measure/condition/timestamp), unit, and a quality state (ok/empty/constant). Concepts are detected by name+value rules (the demographic concepts via data_check_demographic()); when llm_use(TRUE) the model fills concepts and measurement levels the rules left blank. Qualtrics survey exports are recognised (data_check_is_qualtrics()): their extra header rows are stripped so columns type correctly, and the reserved response-metadata columns (StartDate, Duration, Finished, …) are tagged.

The module defaults to rules-only when llm_use(FALSE): columns the rules cannot resolve (ambiguous 3–20-unique integers, ambiguous character columns) fall back to continuous / text respectively. When llm_use(TRUE), ambiguous columns and unresolved file types (other) are sent to llm() for optional refinement, and every file is assigned a study group (ex1, pilot2, shared, …) so that multi-study repositories can be recognised (used by psychds_check). Without an LLM the study group is left NA (unknown).

Column extraction requires files to be readable from disk. Files fetched by repo_check from OSF/GitHub/etc. without a local copy (file_location is NA) are classified but not column-extracted; pass local_path to point at a downloaded copy of the repository.

For a paper whose repository is on Dryad, this means column extraction never happens unless a Dryad API key is set: Dryad requires one to download file bytes, even from a fully public dataset (dataset listing and metadata do not need one). Without a key, every Dryad file stays classified but un-downloaded, column_n is 0, and a warning naming Dryad and ?dryad_pat/?dryad_auth is included among the download failures. A one-time session message is also shown (see repo_check) the first time a Dryad dataset is encountered with no key set.

Every downloaded text file the authors wrote (code, settings, survey definitions such as Qualtrics .qsf files, documentation, outputs) is searched for keys: API keys, access tokens and private keys, which let anyone who downloads the file use a paid service at the authors’ cost. Of a tabular data file only the column names and first 3 rows of the table as read are searched: its values come from participants, but a key the authors set for a whole survey (as embedded data in a Qualtrics survey flow) is exported in every response. The search uses the rules of the secret scanner betterleaks (https://github.com/betterleaks/betterleaks, MIT licence) of high and medium confidence. A key found turns the traffic light red; the report and the keys table give its file, line and type, and only its beginning. Other files over 25 MB and binary files are not searched. A key is never tested with its provider. Every module’s result has any whole key replaced by “keyremoved” (see module_run()).

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Validation: This module has not been validated. All checks in the data_check module have unknown error rates. Carefully evaluate the output of this module. You can help improve this module by reporting an issue on GitHub.

How Reproducibility Check works

This module assesses whether a paper’s R and Python code can be run on its data. It works in two phases. The static phase (always run) collects the package dependencies the code declares, rewrites the data-file paths that do not work in the repository as the authors organised it, works out the order the scripts must run in, and diagnoses why any referenced input file is unavailable — reporting what a reproduction attempt would involve and where it is likely to break. The execution phase (opt-in, execute = TRUE) then actually runs the code: it copies the repository into a throwaway directory, optionally installs the declared dependencies into a throwaway library, runs each script in order in an isolated subprocess with a per-script timeout, and captures the outcome and full output of each run.

By default (execute = FALSE) the module does not run any code — it performs only static analysis: reading dependencies with the same extractors as code_check, finding the files the code references in the repository, and building a run-order dependency graph. It reports what would be installed and run rather than doing it.

When execute = TRUE, the module additionally runs the downloaded code on your machine — a deliberate, opt-in action gated behind that argument (callr is required for R code). Each script runs in an isolated subprocess so a crash cannot take down the R session; note this isolates crashes, not the filesystem or network. Installs (when install_missing = TRUE) and the throwaway run library never touch your main R library. Each script’s outcome is one of ran_ok, errored, timed_out, dependency_unavailable, not_parsed, run_by_source (an R script another script runs with source(), which is run only inside that script, since on its own it lacks the packages and objects the sourcing script sets up first), run_by_import (a Python file other files import, a module rather than a script, unless it has an if __name__ == "__main__": block), jags_model (a JAGS or BUGS model saved with an R extension, which another script reads) or not_r_content (a file that is not R at all, such as a JSON or HTML dump); these last two are not run and are not failures. python2 marks Python 2 code that was not run. With sandbox = "docker" such code runs with Python 2.7 (option metacheck.docker_image_py2; FALSE turns this off), which modifications records; it is never converted, since that can change results. defines_functions is a file that only defines functions, which is used, not run (table$used_by names the scripts that use it), and not_analysis is a file of software rather than of the analysis: a package’s code, tests, documentation or setup.py, a copy of a tool, a web application, a script a program wrote (table$not_analysis_why says which); neither is run or counted as a script. docker_failed means the container never started, which says nothing about the script, so it keeps the light from being green but does not make it red. needs_a_person is a script that stopped because it asks a person to choose a file or answer a question, or that starts an app, and needs_internet one that stopped because the network is off in a Docker run, or, with internet = TRUE, could not reach what it asked for; download_too_large is a script stopped for downloading more than the limits allow (see internet), and out_of_memory one that, or a program it started (such as a compiler), ran out of the memory each script may use. All four are treated the same way. not_downloaded is a script that stopped on a file the repository lists but that was not downloaded, such as one over max_file_size: it could not be tried, so it is not counted as a script that did not reproduce (summary_table$repro_not_downloaded_n counts them apart). not_read is a file metacheck could not read, so it was not run; the light is then not green, as the file may read and run on another computer, and table$error_message says why, naming a package that is installed but cannot be loaded on the computer that ran the check. empty is a file with nothing in it, which is not run or counted as a script. For a script that did not run through, table$error_message is its error, short (at most 300 characters): the error line of its output, which names a package that is not there, with the address it asked for when it needed the internet, or the time limit it reached; table$reason sorts it into a kind. A timeout means “still running at the cutoff”, not a failure, so raise timeout for legitimately long-running (e.g. Bayesian) scripts. dependency_unavailable means the script’s own package could not be installed from live CRAN, the CRAN Archive, or a named GitHub/URL source — an infrastructure limitation, not a defect in the paper’s code, so unlike errored/timed_out it does not force the traffic light red. The full stdout/stderr of every run is kept in the result’s run_results (the report shows it in per-script dropdowns, capped for readability). The traffic light reflects the STATIC readiness signals by default, but an execution error (errored/timed_out) does recolour it to red — a script that crashes on its own analysis is a genuine reproduction failure. A script that fails with could not find function is run once more with the library() calls the project’s other scripts make added at its top (or the one package that exports the function, or magrittr for %>%), and a script that then runs is reported as reproducible only after that repair.

Dependencies are reported name-only, with the source (CRAN, GitHub, or a base package already shipped with R) — static analysis of the code cannot know which version was used, and running against current package versions is itself informative: a break argues the authors should have pinned versions. When the repository does record versions, a run with recorded_versions = TRUE installs the packages the scripts load at those versions, but it does not change the R version, which remains a limitation of the check.

Run order is derived from read-after-write data dependencies (a script writing a file another reads must run first), source() edges, and numeric filename prefixes as a weak tie-breaker. Ambiguous orderings, dependency cycles, and inputs a script reads but that no earlier script produces (and that are not in the repository) are surfaced as warnings, since these are the situations most likely to make an otherwise-correct reproduction fail.

Python code (.py files, and notebooks and Quarto documents whose code is Python, from which the code is extracted first) goes through the same two phases, reported in its own “Python code” section. Each file is checked for syntax errors with Python’s own parser when a Python interpreter (python, or else python3) is available; dependencies are read from the import statements, with versions from a requirements.txt or pyproject.toml when there is one, and installed with pip; each script runs as its own python process (processx is required), so unlike R scripts, a name one script defines is not available to another. When no Python interpreter is available, the Python files are reported as not run and the R results are still returned. Statistics the Python scripts print are compared with the paper together with the R output and any self-reproducible output (JASP/jamovi, SPSS-Viewer, notebook outputs).

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens

Read more in the metacheck manual.

How PubPeer Comments works

This module checks references and warns for citations that have comments on pubpeer (excluding Statcheck comments).

The PubPeer module uses the PubPeer API to check for each reference that has a DOI whether there are comments on the post-publication peer review platform. If comments are found, a link to the comments is provided. Comments by ‘Statcheck’ on PubPeer are ignored, see https://retractionwatch.com/2016/09/02/heres-why-more-than-50000-psychology-studies-are-about-to-have-pubpeer-entries/.

The module requires that the reference has a DOI. If you run the doi_check module in a pipeline before this, it will use the enhanced DOI list from that module, otherwise it will only run on references with existing DOIs.

For more information, see PubPeer.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Lisa DeBruine

Read more in the metacheck manual.

How RetractionWatch works

This module checks references and warns for citations in the RetractionWatch Database.

The RetractionWatch Check module compares the reference list against studies in the RetractionWatch database based on the DOI. If a study in the database is found, a reminder is provided that the study was retracted, has an expression of concern, or a correction.

The module requires that the reference has a DOI. If you run the ref_doi_check module in a pipeline before this, it will use the enhanced DOI list from that module, otherwise it will only run on references with existing DOIs.

It is possible the authors are already aware that a study was retracted, but the module can’t evaluate this.

The database can be manually updated with the rw_update function. For more information, see https://gitlab.com/crossref/retraction-watch-data.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens and Lisa DeBruine

Read more in the metacheck manual.

How Replication Check works

This module checks references and warns for citations of original studies for which replication or reproduction studies exist in the FLoRA database.

The Replication Check module compares the reference list against studies in the FLoRA (FORRT Library of Replication Attempts) database based on the DOI. If a study in the database is found, a reminder is provided that a replication or reproduction of the original study exists, and should be cited (currently, a warning is provided regardless of whether the replication/reproduction study is already cited).

The module requires that the reference has a DOI. If you run the ref_doi_check module in a pipeline before this, it will use the enhanced DOI list from that module, otherwise it will only run on references with existing DOIs.

It is possible the original study was cited for other reasons than the empirical claim tested, or that the replication/reproduction in the FLoRA database is for only one of the studies in the paper, and not the study the authors discuss.

The database can be manually updated with the FLoRA_update() function. For more information, see https://forrt.org/FLoRA/.

This module was developed by Daniel Lakens, Lisa DeBruine and Lukas Wallrich

Read more in the metacheck manual.

Full text

Abstract

Many environmental problems, from climate change to resource depletion, are intergenerational dilemmas: the present generation bears conservation costs, whereas the benefits go to future generations, who can neither observe nor reciprocate. Reputation reliably sustains cooperation among contemporaries, but whether it works across generations remains unclear. In two experiments using an intergenerational goods game, we examined how the audience of disclosure and the type of information disclosed affect contributions to a resource passed from one generation to the next, and whether social value orientation (SVO) moderates these effects.

In Experiment 1, disclosing to the next generation what participants had contributed increased the rate of sustained generations but did not reliably increase individual contributions or narrow the gap between prosocial and proself individuals. In Experiment 2, disclosing the same information to present peers increased both individual contributions and the rate of sustained generations. Adding names and faces was tentatively associated with a further increase in contributions, although this condition was recruited in a separate wave. These gains came mainly from proself individuals, whose contributions no longer differed significantly from those of prosocial individuals when names and faces were disclosed. Findings suggest that disclosure promotes intergenerational cooperation chiefly when it reaches an audience present at the time of decision, and that disclosure, especially identifiable disclosure, matters most for those least motivated to cooperate. Making conservation visible to future generations may not be enough; it may also need to be visible to someone now, suggesting a potentially low-cost approach to promoting environmental cooperation.

1. Introduction

Humanity is currently facing a series of severe global challenges, including climate change, biodiversity loss, and resource depletion (United Nations Environment Programme [UNEP], 2025). These problems pose a serious threat to ecological stability and human wellbeing, and addressing them requires sustained intergenerational cooperation, in which the present generation forgoes immediate benefits to conserve resources for future generations. Unlike cooperation among contemporaries, intergenerational cooperation is temporally asymmetric: The present generation bears the costs, whereas future generations reap the benefits, creating an inherent conflict of interest between present decision makers and future stakeholders (Wade-Benzoni & Tost, 2009). Moreover, because people today cannot directly experience the needs of future generations and need not worry about being held accountable by them, cooperation with the future is fragile: Even when most people conserve, a self-interested minority can be enough to deplete a shared resource (Hauser et al., 2014; Lohse & Waichman, 2020), especially when earlier generations have already acted unsustainably (Shahen et al., 2021).

How, then, can intergenerational cooperation be promoted? A Chinese saying holds that a good name lasts for a hundred generations, suggesting that reputation may reach across generations. Research on cooperation among contemporaries has shown that reputation can sustain cooperation when individuals' decisions are visible to others who can later respond to them: people preferentially help those who have been generous in the past (Wedekind & Milinski, 2000), and the need to maintain such a reputation keeps contributions to a public good high, whereas contributions drop quickly once no further opportunities for reciprocation are expected (Milinski et al., 2002). In addition, previous studies suggest that people are willing to make sacrifices for future generations partly because they wish to leave a positive legacy and to be remembered favorably (Wade-Benzoni & Tost, 2009; Zaval et al., 2015). Because legacy concerns are about how one will be seen by others, they can be regarded as a form of reputational concern that extends across generations.

A key question in intergenerational dilemmas is to whom reputational information should be disclosed for it to constrain behavior. The most obvious candidate audience is future generations. Because future generations ultimately inherit the outcomes of intergenerational cooperation, they are the evaluators with the greatest stake in the present generation's behavior, and people do care about how they will be remembered by future generations (Mah et al., 2024; Wade-Benzoni, 2019; Zaval et al., 2015). However, future generations are an absent audience: They cannot observe individuals' decisions as they are made, nor can they respond in real time.

Experiment 1 tested whether such an audience can still exert enough reputational pressure to promote intergenerational cooperation.

Apart from future generations, members of one's own generation are another natural reputational audience. Previous research has shown that when individuals realize that their behavior will be observed and evaluated by their contemporaries, they often adjust their decisions to maintain a good reputation (Milinski et al., 2002; Wedekind & Milinski, 2000), and some even cooperate without checking the costs in order to appear trustworthy (Jordan et al., 2016). Compared with future generations, members of the same generation are real observers who are present when decisions are made and can observe and evaluate them immediately; in theory, this presence may make reputational pressure more effective. Yet no study has directly tested whether disclosing decisions to peers can promote intergenerational cooperation, and whether it might even do so more effectively than disclosure to future generations. Experiment 2 tested this possibility and then asked whether the effect could be strengthened.

Beyond the audience, individuals' reputational concern may also depend on how much of their identity information is disclosed. Reputational information can include more than the behavior itself: how observable an act is depends both on what is revealed about the actor's behavior and on what is revealed about the actor's identity, such as their name and facial photograph (Bradley et al., 2018). Christens et al. (2019) found that, compared with an anonymous condition, revealing the identities of individual decision makers increased their contributions in a public goods game, an effect the authors attributed to individuals' sensitivity to others' opinions; Sampaio et al. (2023) further showed that information about co-players' identities affected participants' cooperation only when it was presented together with information about those co-players' past behavior. Building on these findings, Experiment 2 also tested whether intergenerational cooperation would receive an additional boost when the type of information disclosed was expanded (from disclosing decisions only to disclosing both decisions and identity information).

However, individuals may respond differently to the same reputational cues. Social value orientation (SVO), a relatively stable personality trait, distinguishes prosocial individuals, who care more about collective welfare, from proself individuals, who care more about their own interests (Murphy et al., 2011; Pletzer et al., 2018; Van Lange et al., 1997). Research on prosocial behavior among contemporaries has found that egoistic (proself) individuals behave generously mainly when their behavior is public and can therefore be reciprocated by others, whereas altruistic (prosocial) individuals behave generously regardless of whether such reputational incentives are present (Simpson & Willer, 2008). One explanation lies in the different sources of cooperative motivation in the two types of individuals: Prosocial individuals' cooperation is driven more by relatively stable intrinsic motives, so they are more likely to cooperate voluntarily even without external pressure; proself individuals, in contrast, rely more on weighing the external costs and benefits of the situation, so their decisions tend to be more sensitive to external signals (Simpson & Willer, 2008; Sun & Ling, 2023; Van Lange et al., 2013; Wu et al., 2016). We therefore measured SVO and examined whether proself individuals would adjust their behavior in response to changes in reputational cues and thereby come closer to prosocial individuals in intergenerational cooperation.

In sum, using the intergenerational goods game (IGG) as adapted by Lohse and Waichman (2020)

2. Experiment 1

Experiment 1 used the IGG paradigm with two conditions: an anonymous condition and a future-generation decision disclosure condition. In the anonymous condition, participants' token contributions were kept completely confidential. In the future-generation decision disclosure condition, following previous manipulations of reputational cues (Arfer et al., 2015), participants were told before making their contribution decisions that their decisions would be disclosed to >Method

2.1.1. Participants

Following Lohse and Waichman (2020), each generational sequence comprised four generations, and each condition started with 18 first-generation groups. Only participants in the first three generations actually made decisions, and the next generation was recruited only if the previous generation had passed the pool on; after the third generation had made its decisions, its contributions determined whether the pool would be passed on to the fourth generation, for which no participants were recruited. A total of 183 participants were included in the anonymous and future-generation decision disclosure (hereafter, future-generation disclosure) conditions: 75 in the anonymous condition (69 women; Mage = 20.27 years, SD = 1.48) and 108 in the futuregeneration disclosure condition (94 women; Mage = 20.10 years, SD = 1.18). Because the total sample size depended on how many pools were passed on and could not be fixed in advance, we conducted a sensitivity power analysis instead of an a priori power analysis in G*Power 3.1 (Faul et al., 2007 (Faul et al., , 2009; ANOVA: Fixed effects, special, main effects and interactions). With α = .05, a total sample size of N = 183, four groups, and a numerator df of 1, the design had 80% power to detect effects of at least f = 0.21 (approximately ηp² = .04) for the main effect of disclosure condition and its interaction with SVO; with 90% and 95% power, the minimum detectable effects were f = 0.24 and 0.27, respectively. Because cell sizes were unequal, these values are approximate. All participants had normal or corrected-to-normal vision and no history of psychiatric disorders. They signed an informed consent form before the experiment and were paid after it. The study was approved by the local ethics committee. Data were collected in spring 2024.

2.1.2. Materials

Social Value Orientation. SVO was measured with the six primary items of the SVO Slider Measure developed by Murphy et al. (2011), each of which offers nine options for allocating money between oneself and another person. The SVO angle was computed as SVO° = arctan [(Āo -50)/(Ās -50)], where Āo and Ās are the mean allocations to the other and to the self, respectively, across the six items. Following the classification criteria of Murphy et al. (2011), participants with SVO angles ≥ 22.45° were classified as prosocial and those with angles < 22.45° as proself; in this experiment, two participants were competitive (< -12.04°) and were included in the proself category.

Reputation Manipulation Check.

To check the effectiveness of the disclosure manipulation, we followed the procedure of Zhan et al. (2022). After making their contribution decisions, participants answered the question "In the situation you just experienced, how much do you think your reputation will be affected?" on a 9-point scale (1 = not affected at all,9 = extremely affected).

2.1.3. Intergenerational Goods Game

We used the IGG as adapted by Lohse and Waichman (2020). The task was presented as a generational sequence: Participants were told that each sequence involved 12 participants randomly assigned to four three-person groups, that each group represented one generation, and that the generations would make their decisions one after another. Each participant was endowed with 10 tokens and decided how many of them (0-10) to contribute to a common resource pool.

If the total contribution of the three group members reached the contribution threshold (≥ 15 tokens), the pool was replenished to its initial level and passed on to the next generation, whose three members likewise each received 10 tokens and made their decisions; if the total fell short of 15 tokens, the sequence ended immediately and all later generations received nothing.

Participants were told that the tokens they kept would be converted into payment at a fixed rate.

Unlike Lohse and Waichman (2020), who informed participants of their position in the generational sequence, we did not reveal to participants which generation they were in; instead, all participants were told that their group was neither the first nor the last. This was intended to place all participants in the same psychological position: Participants at the start of a sequence have no preceding generation from which to inherit the pool, and those at the end have no subsequent generation to pass it on to, so the decision situations they face differ substantially from those of the middle generations. With this adjustment, every participant was, by design, both a recipient and a transmitter of the resource. Three generations were actually run, and the contributions of the third generation determined whether the sequence would continue to the fourth generation. Schematic Diagram of the Intergenerational Goods Game (IGG) Paradigm Note. (A) The pool is passed on to the next generation. (B) The pool is not passed on.

2.1.4. Procedure

At the beginning of the experiment, the experimenter explained the rules of the IGG in detail and told participants that they would complete the task together with two other participants whom they did not know. Before the formal task, participants answered comprehension questions to confirm that they fully understood the task; if they answered incorrectly, the experimenter explained the rules again until they passed the comprehension check. In addition, all participants were told that their final earnings depended on the decisions of the previous group and on their own decisions, and that their own decisions would also affect the earnings of the next group.

In the IGG, participants first made their contribution decisions and completed the reputation manipulation check. They then saw a waiting screen with the message "Collecting the results of the other group members. Please wait…" After the wait, participants received feedback, and the information they saw differed depending on the disclosure condition. There were two disclosure conditions in this experiment. In the anonymous condition, participants saw only their group's total contribution and whether the pool would be passed on to the next generation; no member's individual contribution was shown. Before the task, participants were explicitly told that their individual contributions would be kept completely confidential. In the future-generation disclosure condition, participants saw, in addition to the information above, their own contribution and the contribution of each of the other two group members. Before the task, participants were likewise explicitly told that their individual contributions, together with those of the other two members, would be shown to the three members of the next generation.

Participants then completed the SVO measure.

2.1.5. Data Analysis

Statistical analyses were conducted in R using the car and emmeans packages. All tests were two-tailed, with α = .05. Because cell sizes were unequal, analyses of variance (ANOVAs) used Type III sums of squares with effect coding; marginal means for main effects are reported as estimated marginal means (EMMs) with their standard errors (SEs). For F tests, we report partial eta squared (ηp² ) with its 90% confidence interval (CI ; Steiger, 2004). Pairwise comparisons are reported as mean differences (Mdiff) with 95% CIs, and multiple comparisons were Bonferroni corrected. Cohen's d was computed as the difference between EMMs divided by the square root of the mean squared error (√MSE); in this experiment, MSE = 2.40. The reputation manipulation check was analyzed with Welch's t test (Delacre et al., 2017), and d was computed using the pooled standard deviation. For chi-square tests, we report Cramér's V.

Simple effects were analyzed only when the interaction was significant.

2.2. Results

To check the reputation manipulation, we compared participants' ratings of how much their reputation would be affected between the anonymous and future-generation disclosure conditions using an independent-samples t test (Welch's test; Delacre et al., 2017). Participants in the future-generation disclosure condition perceived a significantly greater impact on their reputation (M = 5.96, SD = 2.15) than those in the anonymous condition (M = 3.63, SD = 1.85), t(172.84) = 7.85, p < .001, d = 1.15, 95% CI [0.83, 1.46], indicating that the reputation manipulation was successful. Because SVO was measured after the task, we further tested whether it was affected by the disclosure condition. The proportion of prosocial individuals did not differ significantly between the anonymous (52.0%) and future-generation disclosure (63.0%) conditions, χ 2 (1, N = 183) = 2.19, p = .139, V = .11, nor did SVO angles, F(1, 181) = 2.69, p = .103, ηp² = .015, 90% CI [.000,.056].

2.2.1. Token Contributions

To examine how disclosure to future generations affected the cooperation of individuals with different SVOs, we conducted a 2 (disclosure condition: anonymous, future-generation disclosure) × 2 (SVO: prosocial, proself) between-subjects ANOVA on token contributions; contributions in each condition are presented in Table 1. The main effect of SVO was significant, F(1, 179) = 31.28, p < .001, ηp² = .149, 90% CI [.076, .227]: Prosocial individuals (EMM = 5.28, SE = 0.16) contributed significantly more than proself individuals (EMM = 3.96, SE = 0.18). The main effect of disclosure condition was not significant, F(1, 179) = 2.44, p = .120, ηp² = .013, 90% CI [.000, .054]; token contributions did not differ significantly between the anonymous condition and the future-generation disclosure condition. The Disclosure Condition × SVO interaction was not significant, F(1, 179) = 0.06, p = .813, ηp² < .001, 90% CI [.000, .015] (see Figure 2). Token Contributions of Prosocial and Proself Individuals Under Each Disclosure Condition in Experiment 1 14 Note. Split violins show the kernel density distribution of each group, points and error bars show cell means and standard errors, and the dashed line marks the fair share (5 tokens). Blue represents prosocial individuals, and red represents proself individuals.

2.2.2. Rate of Sustained Generations

Following previous research (Hauser et al., 2014; Lohse & Waichman, 2020), we also computed two indices of sustained generations at the group level. With the three-person group as the unit of analysis, a group was counted as having passed the pool on to the next generation when its members' total contribution reached the contribution threshold (≥ 15 tokens) and as having failed otherwise. Aggregating across generations, the rate of sustained generations was the proportion of all potential future generations (Generations 2-4 of each generational sequence; 54 per condition) that were sustained, and the conditional rate of sustained generations was the proportion of groups that passed the pool on among all groups that made decisions, which corresponds to the conditional likelihood reported by Lohse and Waichman (2020). To examine whether disclosure to future generations increased the rate of sustained generations at the group level, we conducted chi-square tests on both indices. The rate of sustained generations was significantly higher in the future-generation disclosure condition (42.6%, 23 of 54 potential future generations) than in the anonymous condition (14.8%, 8 of 54), χ 2 (1, N = 108) = 10.18, p = .001, V = .31. The conditional rate of sustained generations showed the same pattern (63.9%, 23 of 36 groups, vs. 32.0%, 8 of 25 groups), χ 2 (1, N = 61) = 6.00, p = .014, V = .31. The rate of sustained generations in the anonymous condition (14.8%) closely matched the no-punishment baseline reported by Lohse and Waichman (2020; 14%), suggesting that our adaptation reproduces the baseline of the laboratory IGG.

In addition, to examine the effect of future-generation disclosure over a longer time horizon, we ran bootstrapping simulations on the observed decisions, following Hauser et al. (2014). Specifically, we simulated 10,000 pools for each condition. In each generation, the contributions of the three members were randomly sampled, with replacement, from the decisions of all participants in that condition; if the total contribution of that generation reached the contribution threshold (≥ 15 tokens), the pool was sustained and passed on to the next generation, and otherwise the pool was depleted and the simulation ended. Each simulation ran for up to 15 generations, and we recorded the proportion of pools sustained in each generation (see Figure 3). In the anonymous condition, pools were depleted rapidly: Only 16.4% of the pools were still sustained after two generations, falling to 1.0% after five generations, and almost all pools were depleted after nine generations. In the future-generation disclosure condition, pools were sustained considerably longer: 43.6% after two generations and 12.0% after five generations, with 0.2% still sustained after 15 generations. It should be noted that this simulation was extrapolated from the observed data and involved no statistical tests; the differences between conditions reflect only the overall trends shown by the simulation. Simulated Long-Term Trends in the Number of Pools Still Sustained in Experiment 1 Note. For each condition, 10,000 pools were simulated; contributions in each generation were drawn with replacement from the actual decisions of participants in that condition, and the simulation was run up to Generation 15. The x-axis shows the generation, and the y-axis shows the number of pools still sustained (out of an initial 10,000). Values at Generation n indicate the pools still sustained after n generations had made their decisions.

2.3. Discussion

Experiment 1 examined whether disclosing the decisions of members of the current generation to future generations promotes intergenerational cooperation. At the individual level, the number of tokens that participants contributed to the pool did not differ significantly between the future-generation disclosure and anonymous conditions, and future-generation disclosure did not narrow the gap in contributions between prosocial and proself individuals. At the group level, both indices of sustained generations were higher in the future-generation disclosure condition than in the anonymous condition, suggesting that future-generation disclosure improved the sustainability of intergenerational resources at the group level.

Taken together, disclosure to future generations did promote intergenerational cooperation to some extent, but the effect was limited. The two measures yielded different results because of the way the rate of sustained generations is determined. This measure is based on whether a group's total contribution reaches the contribution threshold, which makes it a binary measure with the threshold as its cutoff. In both conditions of this experiment, participants' mean contributions were slightly below the fair share of 5 tokens per person, so groups' total contributions were generally close to the threshold (13.40 tokens in the anonymous condition and 14.92 tokens in the future-generation disclosure condition). When most groups' totals hover around the threshold, small and nonsignificant increases in individual contributions, once summed at the group level, can be enough to push some groups that would otherwise have fallen just short over the threshold, producing an observable difference in the group-level measure. The results at the two levels are therefore not contradictory; rather, they are consistent with a small effect of future-generation disclosure that was not strong enough to show up reliably in individuals' contribution decisions.

This limited effect may be related to the audience's distance in time. The constraining effect of reputation on behavior depends on the audience being able to observe individuals' decisions and to evaluate and respond to them accordingly (Milinski et al., 2002; Wedekind & Milinski, 2000). Future generations, however, live long after present decision makers and can neither observe their decisions as they happen nor respond to them. The absence of the audience may have weakened the reputational pressure that disclosure could exert: Although futuregeneration disclosure significantly increased participants' ratings of how much their reputation would be affected (d = 1.15), this perception did not translate into a significant increase in individual contributions.

Experiment 2 therefore turned to another potential audience, peers who are present when decisions are made, to examine whether disclosing decisions to them promotes intergenerational cooperation. Because disclosing decisions alone conveys behavioral information while leaving the actor unidentified (Bradley et al., 2018), Experiment 2 also tested whether adding names and faces was associated with a further increase.

3. Experiment 2

Experiment 2 used the same IGG paradigm as Experiment 1 but changed the audience from future generations to present peers and included three conditions. Specifically, in the anonymous condition, participants' token contributions were kept completely confidential (as in the anonymous condition of Experiment 1); in the peer decision disclosure condition, participants' contributions were shown to the other members of their generation; and in the peer name-and-face disclosure condition, participants' names and faces were shown to the other members of their generation along with their contributions. This experiment thus tested whether, compared with anonymity, disclosing decisions to present peers increases intergenerational cooperation and whether additionally disclosing names and faces brings a further increase.

Experiment 2 also compared the intergenerational cooperation of prosocial and proself individuals across the three conditions to examine whether, as more information is disclosed, the cooperation of proself individuals approaches that of prosocial individuals and the gap between them narrows accordingly.

3.1. Method

>Method

3.1.1. Participants

Participants in Experiment 2 were recruited and selected in the same way as in Experiment 1. The first generation again consisted of 18 groups, and the next generation was recruited only if the previous generation had passed the pool on; three generations were actually run. In total, 354 participants were included in the anonymous, peer decision disclosure (hereafter, peer disclosure), and peer name-and-face disclosure (hereafter, name-and-face disclosure) conditions: 75 in the anonymous condition (69 women; Mage = 20.27 years, SD = 1.48; these participants were drawn from Experiment 1), 132 in the peer disclosure condition Fixed effects, special, main effects and interactions). With α = .05, a total sample size of N = 354, six groups, and a numerator df of 2, the design had 80% power to detect effects of at least f = 0.17 (approximately ηp² = .03), which can serve as an approximate reference for the three-level main effect and its interaction with the other factor; with 90% and 95% power, the minimum detectable effects were approximately f = 0.19 and 0.21, respectively. Because cell sizes were unequal, these values are approximate. Because of the different recruitment waves, participants in the name-and-face disclosure condition were older than those in the other two conditions, F(2, 350) = 103.19, p < .001, ηp² = .371. The proportion of women also differed across the three conditions (92.0%, 83.3%, and 70.1%, respectively), χ 2 (2, N = 354) = 16.50, p < .001, V = .22.

We therefore repeated the analyses with gender (Model 1) and both gender and age (Model 2) as covariates. Crucially, in both models, all main effects and the Disclosure Condition × SVO interaction remained significant, and the pattern of simple effects and interaction contrasts was largely unchanged when controlling for age and gender. The only exception was the difference between the name-and-face and peer disclosure conditions, which was not significant after Bonferroni correction in either model (Model 1: p = .067; Model 2: Mdiff = 0.25, p = .802).

Importantly, because age was almost completely confounded with the name-and-face disclosure condition, covariate adjustment cannot fully separate their effects (Miller & Chapman, 2001), and this specific comparison should therefore be interpreted with caution. Age data for one participant were treated as missing because of an entry error, so age statistics are based on 353 participants. Informed consent, payment, and ethics approval were the same as in Experiment 1.

3.1.2. Materials

The reputation manipulation check, the SVO measure, and the experimental paradigm were the same as in Experiment 1. In this experiment, one altruistic participant (> 57.15°) was included in the prosocial category, and three competitive participants (< -12.04°) were included in the proself category. The face stimuli were prepared following the general procedure of Gong et al. (2011): Eight undergraduates (four men and four women) were recruited from a university, and photographs of their neutral facial expressions were taken under standardized conditions.

Another 30 raters (25 women; age range = 19-25 years, M = 22.70, SD = 2.04) rated each photograph on attractiveness, emotional arousal, trustworthiness, and emotional valence (positive-negative) on 7-point scales. Interrater reliability was estimated with ICC(2,k), based on a mean-rating, absolute-agreement, two-way random-effects model; the ICCs were .96, .91, .67, and .92 for the four dimensions, respectively. Four of the faces (two male and two female) were selected: Faces 2 and 4 were used in the example disclosure screen shown before the decision, and Faces 3 and 5 were used in the feedback screen shown after the decision. The two faces used in the predecision example were rated below the scale midpoint (4) on attractiveness and arousal and did not differ significantly from the midpoint on trustworthiness, whereas the two faces used in the postdecision feedback were rated above the midpoint on trustworthiness. For the name stimuli, two common Chinese names (Ding Wenrui and Huang Xinqian) were selected on the basis of expert ratings.

3.1.3. Procedure

The procedure was largely the same as in Experiment 1. The difference was that, in the peer disclosure and name-and-face disclosure conditions, participants were told that their decisions and related information would be disclosed to the other two members of their group.

Specifically, in the name-and-face disclosure condition, participants entered their names at the start of the experiment and had a photograph of their face taken and uploaded on site. Before making their contribution decisions, participants were told that, after this round of contributions, their contribution, name, and photograph would be disclosed to the other two group members and that they would also see the other two members' results; they were then shown an example of the disclosure screen, which displayed two faces (Faces 2 and 4), with the names and contributions replaced by the placeholders "Zhang San" and "Li Si" and "X" and "Y," respectively. After making their contribution decisions and completing the reputation manipulation check, participants saw the names (Ding Wenrui and Huang Xinqian), faces (Faces 3 and 5), and token contributions of the other two members, and then completed the SVO measure.

3.1.4. Data Analysis

Data were analyzed in the same way as in Experiment 1; in this experiment, MSE = 2.20.

Simple effects and pairwise comparisons were all based on the error term of the full model.

Pairwise comparisons between disclosure conditions, both overall and within proself individuals, were Bonferroni corrected (k = 3); comparisons between prosocial and proself individuals within each disclosure condition were single within-condition comparisons and were not corrected; interaction contrasts and the chi-square tests on contribution strategies were Bonferroni corrected (k = 3).

3.2. Results

To check whether the two disclosure manipulations were effective, we compared each disclosure condition with the anonymous condition using independent-samples t tests (Welch's test; Delacre et al., 2017). Ratings of how much participants' reputation would be affected were significantly higher in the peer disclosure condition (M = 6.54, SD = 1.76) than in the anonymous condition (M = 3.63, SD = 1.85), t(147.41) = 11.08, p < .001, d = 1.62, 95% CI [1.30, 1.95], and ratings in the name-and-face disclosure condition (M = 4.77, SD = 2.10) were also significantly higher than in the anonymous condition, t(166.70) = 4.15, p < .001, d = 0.57, 95% CI [0.28, 0.85]. These results indicate that both forms of disclosure increased participants' perception that their reputation could be affected, confirming that the reputation manipulation was successful. In addition, the proportion of prosocial individuals did not differ significantly across the three conditions (52.0%, 59.1%, and 60.5%, respectively), χ 2 (2, N = 354) = 1.56, p = .458, V = .07, nor did SVO angles, F(2, 351) = 0.95, p = .388, ηp² = .005, 90% CI [.000, .021].

3.2.1. Token Contributions

Token contributions in each condition of Experiment 2 are presented in Table 2. To examine how the disclosure conditions affected the intergenerational cooperation of individuals with different SVOs, we conducted a 3 (disclosure condition: anonymous, peer disclosure, nameand-face disclosure) × 2 (SVO: prosocial, proself) between-subjects ANOVA on token contributions. The main effect of disclosure condition was significant, F(2, 348) = 16.41, p < .001, ηp² = .086, 90% CI [.042, .133]. Bonferroni-corrected pairwise comparisons showed that token contributions were significantly higher in the peer disclosure condition than in the anonymous condition, Mdiff = 0.78, 95% CI [0.26, 1.30], F(1, 348) = 12.95, p = .001, d = 0.52, and also significantly higher in the name-and-face disclosure condition than in the anonymous condition, Mdiff = 1.22, 95% CI [0.71, 1.73], F(1, 348) = 32.80, p < .001, d = 0.82. Contributions in the name-and-face disclosure condition were also significantly higher than in the peer disclosure condition, Mdiff = 0.44, 95% CI [0.00, 0.88], F(1, 348) = 5.85, p = .048, d = 0.30. The main effect of SVO was significant, F(1, 348) = 18.87, p < .001, ηp² = .051, 90% CI [.020, .093]: Prosocial individuals (EMM = 5.47, SE = 0.11) contributed significantly more than proself individuals (EMM = 4.74, SE = 0.12). The Disclosure Condition × SVO interaction was significant, F(2, 348) = 4.03, p = .019, ηp² = .023, 90% CI [.002, .051]. Because the interaction was significant, we conducted simple effects analyses. Among proself individuals, the simple effect of disclosure condition was significant, F(2, 348) = 16.70, p < .001, ηp² = .088, 90% CI [.043, .134].

Bonferroni-corrected pairwise comparisons showed that proself individuals contributed significantly more in the peer disclosure condition than in the anonymous condition, Mdiff = 1.16, 95% CI [0.39, 1.93], F(1, 348) = 13.14, p < .001, d = 0.78, and also significantly more in the name-and-face disclosure condition than in the anonymous condition, Mdiff = 1.82, 95% CI [1.06, 2.58], F(1, 348) = 33.37, p < .001, d = 1.23. Their contributions in the name-and-face disclosure condition were numerically higher than in the peer disclosure condition, Mdiff = 0.66, 95% CI [-0.01, 1.34], d = 0.45, but this difference was not significant after correction, F(1, 348) = 5.56, p = .057. Among prosocial individuals, by contrast, the simple effect of disclosure condition was not significant, F(2, 348) = 2.33, p = .099, ηp² = .013, 90% CI [.000, .036]. In addition, prosocial individuals contributed significantly more than proself individuals in the anonymous condition, Mdiff = 1.38, 95% CI [0.70, 2.05], F(1, 348) = 16.15, p < .001, d = 0.93, and still contributed significantly more in the peer disclosure condition, Mdiff = 0.62, 95% CI [0.10, 1.13], F(1, 348) = 5.54, p = .019, d = 0.42, whereas the difference between the two was not significant in the nameand-face disclosure condition, Mdiff = 0.17, 95% CI [-0.32, 0.67], F(1, 348) = 0.48, p = .491, d = 0.12 (see Figure 4). Further interaction contrasts (Bonferroni corrected, k = 3) showed that the gap in contributions between prosocial and proself individuals was significantly smaller in the name-and-face disclosure condition than in the anonymous condition (difference in gaps = 1.21, adjusted 95% CI [0.18, 2.23], t(348) = 2.84 , p = .014); none of the other pairwise comparisons were significant (ps > .23).
