3.2.2. Contribution Strategies

To further examine how the disclosure conditions affected the decision strategies of individuals with different SVOs, we classified each participant's contribution as free riding (< 5 tokens), precise cooperation (= 5 tokens), or supercooperation (> 5 tokens). We then conducted chi-square tests in two directions, comparing the strategy distributions of prosocial and proself individuals within each disclosure condition and comparing the strategy distributions across the three disclosure conditions within each SVO subgroup (all p values were Bonferroni corrected, k = 3). Comparisons within each disclosure condition showed that, in the anonymous condition, prosocial and proself individuals differed significantly in the proportion of free riding (23.1% vs. 58.3%), χ 2 (1, N = 75) = 9.70, p = .006, V = .36, and in the proportion of supercooperation (43.6% vs. 11.1%), χ 2 (1, N = 75) = 9.80, p = .005, V = .36, but not in the proportion of precise cooperation (33.3% vs. 30.6%), χ 2 (1, N = 75) = 0.07, p = 1.000, V = .03. In the peer disclosure condition, the two still differed significantly in the proportion of supercooperation (48.7% vs. 18.5%), χ 2 (1, N = 132) = 12.58, p = .001, V = .31, but no longer differed significantly in the proportion of free riding (11.5% vs. 27.8%), χ 2 (1, N = 132) = 5.66, p = .052, V = .21, and again did not differ significantly in the proportion of precise cooperation (39.7% vs. 53.7%), χ 2 (1, N = 132) = 2.51, p = .340, V = .14. In the name-and-face disclosure condition, by contrast, none of the three proportions differed significantly: free riding (4.5% vs. 13.8%), χ 2 (1, N = 147) = 4.05, p = .133, V = .17; precise cooperation (47.2% vs. 44.8%), χ 2 (1, N = 147) = 0.08, p = 1.000, V = .02; and supercooperation (48.3% vs. 41.4%), χ 2 (1, N = 147) = 0.68, p = 1.000, V = .07.

Comparisons across the three disclosure conditions within each SVO subgroup showed that the proportion of free riding among proself individuals differed significantly across conditions, χ 2 (2, N = 148) = 21.25, p < .001, V = .38. Post hoc comparisons showed that the proportions of free riding in the peer disclosure (27.8%) and name-and-face disclosure (13.8%) conditions were both significantly lower than in the anonymous condition (58.3%), χ 2 (1, N = 90) = 8.40, p = .011, V = .31, and χ 2 (1, N = 94) = 20.66, p < .001, V = .47, respectively, whereas the peer disclosure and name-and-face disclosure conditions did not differ significantly, χ 2 (1, N = 112) = 3.35, p = .201, V = .17. The proportion of supercooperation among proself individuals also differed significantly across conditions, χ 2 (2, N = 148) = 12.95, p = .005, V = .30. Post hoc comparisons showed that this proportion was significantly higher in the name-and-face disclosure condition (41.4%) than in the anonymous condition (11.1%), χ 2 (1, N = 94) = 9.73, p = .005, V = .32, and in the peer disclosure condition (18.5%), χ 2 (1, N = 112) = 6.91, p = .026, V = .25, whereas the anonymous and peer disclosure conditions did not differ significantly, χ 2 (1, N = 90) = 0.90, p = 1.000, V = .10. The proportion of precise cooperation among proself individuals did not differ significantly across the three conditions, χ 2 (2, N = 148) = 4.69, p = .288, V = .18. Among prosocial individuals, the proportion of free riding also differed significantly across conditions, χ 2 (2, N = 206) = 9.91, p = .021, V = .22. Post hoc comparisons showed that only the name-and-face disclosure condition (4.5%) had a significantly lower proportion than the anonymous condition (23.1%), χ 2 (1, N = 128) = 10.26, p = .004, V = .28; none of the other pairwise comparisons were significant (ps > .25). Among prosocial individuals, neither the proportion of precise cooperation, χ 2 (2, N = 206) = 2.35, p = .927, V = .11, nor that of supercooperation, χ 2 (2, N = 206) = 0.31, p = 1.000, V = .04, differed significantly across the three conditions.

Overall, the proportion of precise cooperation did not differ significantly in any comparison, and the changes in strategy distributions were concentrated at the two ends, free riding and supercooperation. As more information was disclosed, the proportion of free riders among proself individuals decreased and the proportion of supercooperators increased, so that the strategy distributions of prosocial and proself individuals became more similar, in line with the narrowing gap between them in token contributions (see Figure 5). Distribution of Contribution Strategies by Disclosure Condition and SVO in Experiment 2 Note. The diverging stacked bars are centered on precise cooperation (= 5), with free riding (< 5) extending to the left and supercooperation (> 5) extending to the right. Numbers are percentages.

3.2.3. Rate of Sustained Generations

Both indices of sustained generations were calculated as in Experiment 1. To examine whether the disclosure conditions increased the rate of sustained generations at the group level, we conducted chi-square tests on both indices. The rate of sustained generations differed significantly across the three conditions (anonymous: 14.8%; peer disclosure: 64.8%; name-andface disclosure: 83.3%; each out of 54 potential future generations), χ 2 (2, N = 162) = 54.68, p < .001, Cramér's V = .58. Bonferroni-corrected (k = 3) post hoc pairwise comparisons showed that the anonymous condition differed significantly from both the peer disclosure condition, χ 2 (1, N = 108) = 28.17, p < .001, V = .51, and the name-and-face disclosure condition, χ 2 (1, N = 108) = 50.72, p < .001, V = .69, whereas the peer disclosure and name-and-face disclosure conditions did not differ significantly, χ 2 (1, N = 108) = 4.82, p = .084, V = .21. The conditional rate of sustained generations also differed significantly across the three conditions, χ 2 (2, N = 118) = 32.17, p < .001, Cramér's V = .52. For this index, Bonferroni-corrected (k = 3) post hoc pairwise comparisons showed that the anonymous condition (32.0%, 8 of 25 groups) differed significantly from both the peer disclosure condition (79.5%, 35 of 44 groups), χ 2 (1, N = 69) = 15.35, p < .001, V = .47, and the name-and-face disclosure condition (91.8%, 45 of 49 groups), χ 2 (1, N = 74) = 29.16, p < .001, V = .63, whereas the peer disclosure and name-and-face disclosure conditions did not differ significantly, χ 2 (1, N = 93) = 2.91, p = .264, V = .18.

To examine the effects of the disclosure conditions over a longer time horizon, we used the same simulation method as in Experiment 1, simulating 10,000 pools for each of the three conditions for up to 15 generations (see Figure 6). In the anonymous condition, pools were depleted rapidly: Only 16.4% of the pools were still sustained after two generations, falling to 1.0% after five generations, and almost all pools were depleted after nine generations. In the peer disclosure condition, pools were sustained longer (54.1% after two generations and 22.2% after five generations, with 1.2% still sustained after 15 generations), and in the name-and-face disclosure condition, they were sustained the longest (80.9% after two generations and 59.5% after five generations, with 21.8% still sustained even after 15 generations). As in Experiment 1, this simulation involved no statistical tests, and the differences between conditions reflect only the trends shown by the simulation. Simulated Long-Term Trends in the Number of Pools Still Sustained in Experiment 2 Note. For each condition, 10,000 pools were simulated; contributions in each generation were drawn with replacement from the actual decisions of participants in that condition, and the simulation was run up to Generation 15. The x-axis shows the generation, and the y-axis shows the number of pools still sustained (out of an initial 10,000). Values at Generation n indicate the pools still sustained after n generations had made their decisions.

3.3. Discussion

Experiment 2 examined whether disclosing individuals' decisions promotes intergenerational cooperation when the audience consists of present peers, and whether additionally disclosing identity information such as names and faces strengthens this effect. The results showed that, at the individual level, contributions in both the peer disclosure and nameand-face disclosure conditions were significantly higher than in the anonymous condition, and contributions in the name-and-face disclosure condition were significantly higher than in the peer disclosure condition; proself individuals contributed significantly more in both disclosure conditions than in the anonymous condition, although their contributions did not differ significantly between the two disclosure conditions, and the gap between prosocial and proself individuals narrowed and was no longer significant in the name-and-face disclosure condition.

At the group level, the rate of sustained generations in both disclosure conditions was also significantly higher than in the anonymous condition, but it did not differ significantly between the peer disclosure and name-and-face disclosure conditions. Taken together, when the audience consists of present peers, disclosing decisions can effectively increase intergenerational cooperation, and additionally disclosing names and faces was associated with a further increase.

In contrast to Experiment 1, in which disclosure to future generations did not significantly increase individual contributions, disclosure to present peers in this experiment significantly increased cooperation. Beyond this, name-and-face disclosure was associated with further increases in intergenerational cooperation at the individual level. However, this increase appeared only in individual token contributions and not in the rate of sustained generations. This can be explained by a threshold account: In both conditions with disclosure to peers, participants' mean contributions were already above the fair share of 5 tokens per person, so most groups reached the contribution threshold anyway. In this case, further increases in individual contributions mainly led groups that had already crossed the threshold to contribute more, rather than changing whether groups crossed it, and were therefore unlikely to produce a significant difference in a threshold-based group-level measure. In addition, there were only slightly more than 40 groups per disclosure condition, so the group-level chi-square tests had relatively limited power.

4. General Discussion

Using the IGG as adapted by Lohse and Waichman (2020), the present research examined how two dimensions of reputational disclosure-audience and content-affect intergenerational cooperation. Disclosure to future generations increased the rate of sustained generations but did not significantly increase individual contributions or narrow the gap between prosocial and proself individuals, whereas disclosure to present peers increased both individual contributions and the rate of sustained generations. Adding names and faces was associated with a further increase in contributions and, relative to the anonymous condition, with a significantly narrower prosocial-proself gap; in the name-and-face condition, the gap was no longer significant. Taken together, the findings suggest that the two dimensions play different roles:

Whether the audience is present may determine whether disclosure reliably changes individual decisions, whereas the extent of identity disclosure may shape how strongly disclosure affects proself individuals.

Together, the results of the two experiments suggest that the mere act of disclosure may not be sufficient to promote intergenerational cooperation; its effect depends on the audience to whom the information is directed. This dependence can be understood in terms of how reputation works. In cooperation among contemporaries, reputation may help sustain cooperation through two routes. The first is direct reciprocity, whereby, in repeated interactions, an individual's cooperation may be returned by the partner later on (Axelrod & Hamilton, 1981; Trivers, 1971).

The second is indirect reciprocity, whereby cooperative behavior observed by third parties builds a good reputation, which in turn makes others more willing to cooperate with the individual in the future (Alexander, 1987; Nowak & Sigmund, 1998 , 2005). In intergenerational contexts, the first route may be weakened: Because future generations cannot reciprocate toward the present generation, individuals' sacrifices are unlikely to be repaid through future returns. More importantly, the payoff of the second route may also vary with the type of audience. When information is disclosed to future generations, individuals can anticipate that they will have no further interaction or contact with them, so whatever reputation they build is unlikely to bring any real return. When information is directed at peers, by contrast, individuals may expect some possibility of future interaction with this audience, so a good reputation is more likely to affect future opportunities for cooperation, which makes maintaining one's reputation an investment with potential returns. Our results are consistent with this reasoning: Relative to the same anonymous baseline, disclosing decisions to contemporaries, with whom future interaction was possible, increased individual contributions, whereas disclosing the same decisions to future generations did not. At the level of individual decisions, the audience that mattered was not the generation that would benefit from cooperation but the contemporaries who could observe the decision and respond to it. This suggests that the prospect of future interaction may not be an incidental feature of the reputational mechanism but rather a prerequisite for it to work. This interpretation is consistent with evidence that reputation sustains contributions to a public good only when later opportunities for indirect reciprocity are expected (Milinski et al., 2002; Wu et al., 2016) and that observability promotes prosociality more strongly when decisions can affect how others will later respond (Bradley et al., 2018), although reputational concerns can also restrain selfish behavior when no further economic interaction with the observer is expected (Arfer et al., 2015). Moreover, the present research did not manipulate or measure participants' theory (Meyer et al., 2010). When situational cues are sufficiently explicit and the consequences of behavior are sufficiently clear, preexisting individual differences tend to explain less of the variation in behavior, and behavior is more likely to be governed by the external constraints and incentives provided by the situation. In the present research, as the level of disclosure increased, proself individuals, who rely more on weighing external costs and benefits, were thus more likely to move toward the level of prosocial individuals and to behave more cooperatively (Simpson & Willer, 2008; Van Lange et al., 2013; Wu et al., 2016); meanwhile, prosocial individuals were already cooperating at a high level, so the gap between the two narrowed as disclosure increased. This pattern fits the distinction proposed above: Disclosure, especially identifiable disclosure, appears to matter mainly for those who are least intrinsically motivated to cooperate.

For environmental psychology, the central implication is that making conservation visible on behalf of the future is not the same as making it accountable. In our experiments, disclosure to the next generation was perceived as reputationally consequential but did little to change individual decisions, whereas the same information shown to present peers did. This bears on how disclosure is designed in environmental governance. Many instruments, such as carbon footprint labels, corporate sustainability reports, and intergenerational resource accounts, justify transparency by appeal to future generations, yet their behavioral force may depend on whether a contemporaneous audience (customers, investors, neighbors, or regulators) actually observes and evaluates what is disclosed. Previous work has sustained intergenerational resources through binding median voting (Hauser et al., 2014) and partially improved their sustainability through costly peer punishment within a generation (Lohse & Waichman, 2020); our results suggest that a lower-cost lever, directing disclosure to a present audience and, where privacy and consent whether they crowd out intrinsic environmental motivation, or whether identifiable disclosure provokes reactance and privacy concerns are questions the present data cannot answer but that any intervention recommendation must eventually confront. More broadly, the difficulty with disclosure to future generations lies less in their relevance than in their absence-and that absence is a design problem for institutions, not only for experiments.

5. Conclusions

Intergenerational environmental dilemmas pose a distinctive problem for reputationbased cooperation: the people who benefit from present restraint are not yet there to see it.

Across two experiments, we found that disclosure promoted intergenerational cooperation, but its strength depended on who received the information and what the information revealed.

Disclosing contributions to the next generation helped more groups pass the resource on but did not reliably change individual decisions. Disclosing the same contributions to present peers raised individual contributions as well, and adding names and faces was associated with a further increase, concentrated among proself individuals, to the point that their contributions no longer differed significantly from those of prosocial individuals.

Our results refine the view that reputation sustains cooperation. In intergenerational settings, visibility appears to translate into restraint chiefly when an audience is present to observe and evaluate the decision, and disclosure, especially identifiable disclosure, appears to matter most for those least motivated to cooperate. For environmental communication and disclosure policy, the implication is that transparency directed at posterity is a weak lever on its own. It is more likely to change behavior when paired with contemporaneous accountability and, within the limits of privacy and consent, when those whose decisions affect the future can be identified.

Whether these principles hold beyond the laboratory remains to be established. The next steps are to separate audience presence from the anticipated interaction that accompanies it, to replicate the identity effect in a fully randomized design, and to test disclosure in real environmental decisions with more diverse populations. A particularly promising direction is to give future generations a present voice, through proxies or institutional representatives who can observe and evaluate today's decisions on their behalf, and to ask whether doing so restores the reputational force that distant audiences lack. Evidence that taking the perspective of future generations before deciding reduces unsustainable choices (Shahen et al., 2021) makes this direction worth pursuing.

Data Availability Statement

All data, R analysis scripts, Python figure-generation code, and the resulting output tables and figures are openly available in the Open Science Framework (OSF) at https://osf.io/vk4gq/ expectations of future interaction, and this explanation awaits direct testing in future research.

Given that disclosing decisions to peers does promote intergenerational cooperation, the question that follows is how this effect can be further enhanced. Disclosing decisions alone conveys behavioral information without revealing the actor's identity, whereas adding names and faces links the behavior to a specific, identifiable individual (Bradley et al., 2018). We found that disclosing names and faces in addition to decisions was associated with higher token contributions, in line with previous findings that identifiability promotes cooperation (Christens et al., 2019; Wang et al., 2017) and that information about others' identities matters mainly when it accompanies information about their behavior (Sampaio et al., 2023). A meta-analysis, however, found no reliable difference between manipulations that reveal both identity and behavior and those that reveal behavior alone (Bradley et al., 2018), so this additional benefit should be interpreted with caution, particularly because the name-and-face condition was recruited in a separate wave.

More notably, this increase was not equal across individuals. Prosocial individuals' contributions remained high in all conditions and did not change significantly with disclosure condition, whereas proself individuals contributed significantly more in both disclosure conditions than in the anonymous condition. The gap between the two was significantly narrower under name-and-face disclosure than in the anonymous condition and was no longer significant in that condition. Among proself individuals, strategy changes were concentrated at the two ends: The proportion of free riders decreased and the proportion of supercooperators increased, whereas the proportion of precise cooperators remained largely unchanged. For prosocial individuals, the proportions of precise cooperation and supercooperation did not differ significantly across the three conditions. These results are consistent with situational strength allow, making actors identifiable, may achieve part of the same effect. Practical measures should not stop at disclosure as a formality; they also need to ensure that the disclosed information reaches a present audience that is able to observe and evaluate the behavior, so that reputational pressure can actually take effect. At the same time, provided that regulatory requirements are met and privacy consent is obtained, the identifiability of the information and the extent to which behavior can be attributed to individuals should be moderately increased. Because this effect of disclosure was more pronounced among proself individuals, such interventions may also help, to some extent, narrow differences in cooperation within groups.

Several questions remain open, not as oversights of the present design but as extensions that the design itself was not built to settle. The first concerns what audience presence means in our design. Because future-generation and peer disclosure were tested against a shared anonymous baseline, the contrast between them is indirect, and the two audiences differed in more than their presence at the time of decision: peers were co-actors who faced the same choice, whose own contributions were also disclosed, and with whom participants might have expected further contact, whereas the next generation consisted of recipients whom participants had no reason to expect to meet. Presence in our design is therefore a bundle of featurestemporal co-location, mutual exposure, and the prospect of future interaction-and we cannot say which of them carries the effect; a crossed design that varies these features independently, for example by disclosing decisions to peers who will never be met again, would turn this interpretation into a test. A second limitation concerns the sample: given the constraints, participants were recruited in two waves and had an uneven age and gender composition, and a broader sample is needed to test the boundaries of these conclusions. Finally, we measured only immediate behavior: whether reputation-driven contributions persist once observation ends,

, the present research systematically examined how different forms of reputational disclosure affect the intergenerational cooperation of individuals with different SVOs. The research comprised two experiments. Experiment 1 focused on disclosing the decisions of members of the current generation to future generations and examined whether reputational pressure in an intergenerational context can increase the intergenerational cooperation of proself individuals. Experiment 2 replaced the audience with members of the current generation and manipulated the type of information disclosed to test whether disclosing decisions to peers promotes intergenerational cooperation more effectively; it further tested whether, as more information is disclosed, the intergenerational cooperation of proself individuals comes closer to that of prosocial individuals. By examining who can observe conservation-relevant decisions and what identity information is disclosed, the present research investigates when reputational concerns may help sustain cooperation across generations, offering insight into the psychological processes that can support conservation for future generations.
